The monetary-easing cycle that began in 2024 failed to
produce a meaningful recovery in household borrowing.
Global household-debt growth edged up from +3.1%
in 2024 to +3.4% in 2025, but remained well below its
long-term average of +4.5%. This muted response is
not particularly surprising: long-term interest rates
did not follow policy rates down to the same extent,
leaving mortgages and other long-term loans relatively
expensive. Overall, global household liabilities reached a

new record of EUR56.9trn at the end of 2025 (Figure 15).

Debt growth in 2025 remained highly uneven. The
strongest increases were concentrated in emerging
markets. Liabilities rose by +11.8% in Eastern Europe and
+10.8% in Latin America, while Asia excluding China and

Japan recorded growth of +7.0%. While household debt
growth in advanced economies remained moderate
compared with emerging markets, it accelerated
noticeably across most regions in 2025: In Western
Europe, debt growth nearly doubled from +1.4% in 2024
to +2.7% in 2025, while North America recorded an
acceleration from +2.0% to +3.2%. Australia and New
Zealand followed at +5.9%. Japan and China stood out
in opposite directions. Japanese household liabilities

increased by +2.9%. Excluding the pandemic-distorted
year 2020 (+3.8%), this was the strongest rise this century
– another sign that Japan may finally be leaving its long
deflationary period behind. China, meanwhile, moved
sharply in the opposite direction. After expanding by an
average of +17.5% annually over the past two decades,
household liabilities grew by only +0.5% in 2025 (Figure
16). The debt engine that powered much of China’s past
growth has effectively stalled as the prolonged property

downturn weighs on housing demand and consumer
confidence. What began as a cyclical slowdown
increasingly points to a broader shift away from debt-
driven expansion and towards balance-sheet caution.
Significant shifts in the global debt map have been
caused by the very different long-term growth rates
of private debt (Figure 17). North America’s share of
worldwide household debt has fallen from 47.4% in 2005
to 35.9% in 2025. Meanwhile Western Europe’s share

declined from 33.6% to 23.9% and Japan’s halved from
8.4% to 4.2%. Together, these three traditional debt
centers accounted for only 64% of global liabilities in
2025, compared with almost 90% two decades earlier.
The opposite trend is evident in Asia, particularly China,
whose share of global debt has surged from 1.7% to
18.3%, despite its current weak performance. Chinese
households alone now account for almost 60% of all
private debt in Asia (including Japan). Although China’s

near-stagnant borrowing now marks a sharp break with
its past expansion, the long-term debt story is therefore
one of migration from West to East.

Box 4: Selfish retirees, overburdened youngsters?
The debate about the financial burden demographic change will impose on younger generations is often strikingly
one-sided. Much attention is rightly paid to the rising cost of public pensions, health and long-term care systems as
populations age. But there is another side to the intergenerational balance sheet that receives far less attention: over
the coming decades, younger generations will also inherit an exceptionally large stock of private wealth that has been

accumulated by today’s older generations.
In the USA, households close to or already in retirement held around 86% of total gross financial assets and 80% of net
financial assets⁵ in 2025. Total net financial assets of the 55–69 and 70+ age groups amounted to around EUR80trn,
equivalent to 305% of US GDP . Including real estate and durable consumer goods, their total net assets reached around
EUR109trn. The wealthiest age group was the 55–69 cohort, with gross financial assets of EUR49.2trn and real estate

and durable consumer goods worth EUR16.6trn. This was followed by households aged 70 and older, whose combined
financial and real assets amounted EUR49.6trn (Figure 18).
⁵  See: Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System (2026): DFA: Distributional Financial Account. Distribution of Household Wealth in the U.S.
since 1989, https://www.federalreserve.gov/releases/z1/dataviz/dfa/distribute/table/

For Germany, the financial wealth of households aged 55 and older can only be estimated approximately on the basis

of household surveys. According to the latest Panel on Household Finances (PHF) survey, published by the Bundesbank⁶,
mean gross wealth reached around EUR114,400 for households whose reference person was aged 55–64, EUR98,200
for those aged 65–74 and EUR107,900 for those aged 75 and older. The comparatively lower figure for the 65–74 age
group may partly reflect the weaker labor-market experience of cohorts born between 1950 and 1958, who were
exposed to high unemployment and subdued real wage growth during important parts of their working lives. Combining

these wealth estimates with the number of households in each age group reported in the Mikrozensus⁷ , we estimate
that the cohorts aged 55 and older hold around 60% of the private households’ gross financial assets, which would
correspond to around EUR 6.1trn in 2025 (see Figure 19).
Including real estate, average household net wealth in Germany stood at EUR324,000 in 2023, slightly above the
euro-area average of EUR312,000⁸. Wealth peaked among households whose reference person was aged 55–64, at

EUR440,100 on average. It remained high among those aged 65–74, at EUR411,400, before declining to EUR343,700 for
households aged 75 and older. By contrast, households headed by someone aged 16–34 held average net wealth of just
EUR106,900.
These differences largely reflect the typical lifecycle of wealth accumulation. Households build up assets during their
working lives and, on average, begin to draw them down in retirement. The same pattern can be observed across most

EU countries, although the pace of decumulation in old age differs markedly depending on the generosity and financing
structure of public pension systems (Figure 20).

These figures should nevertheless be interpreted with caution. Household wealth surveys tend to understate financial
assets, while the very wealthiest households are often underrepresented. In Germany, for example, the PHF survey
puts average gross financial assets at EUR87,400 per household, compared with EUR117,890 based on the aggregate
household balance sheet. Survey-based estimates of future inheritances should therefore be regarded as indicative

rather than precise.
Moreover, the wealth that younger generations may inherit over the coming decades represents an upper bound
rather than a guaranteed transfer. Wealth is distributed very unevenly: in Germany, the richest 10% own 53.7% of total
net wealth⁹. DIW estimates suggest that around EUR400bn is inherited or gifted each year, with roughly half of these
transfers accruing to the wealthiest 10% of beneficiaries10. At the same time, rising health and long-term care costs,

together with less generous pension systems, are likely to absorb a growing share of household wealth before it can be
transferred. The coming inheritance wave will therefore be substantial, but highly uneven, and could reinforce existing
wealth inequalities within younger generations.
The same applies in the US. Consider a single retiree who wants to withdraw an average of EUR2,000 per month to
cover living, health and care costs. Assuming average inflation of 3.0%, an annual return of 5.0% and a retirement period

of 17.4 years – the average remaining life expectancy of a 70-year-old woman according to the latest UN population
statistics – the required savings at age 70 would amount to around EUR368,700. This is equivalent to roughly 46% of
the calculated average net wealth of EUR802,800 for the 70+ age group. A substantial share of accumulated wealth
therefore needs to remain available to finance retirement itself rather than being passed on.
Combined with the highly unequal distribution of US household wealth, this helps explain why estimates of the “Great

Wealth Transfer” vary so widely, from around USD36trn to more than USD100trn over the coming decades.11 Headline
asset stocks therefore provide only a rough indication of the wealth that will ultimately be transferred to younger
generations.
Nevertheless, the broader conclusion remains: the intergenerational debate should consider both sides of the ledger.
Younger generations will face rising public-sector burdens as societies age, but they will also receive one of the largest

private wealth transfers in history. Neither offsets the other. But discussing future pension, health and long-term
care liabilities without considering the parallel transfer of private assets gives an incomplete picture of the economic
relationship between generations.

Households deleverage – governments do not
The global household-debt ratio fell to 60.9% of GDP
in 2025, almost 10pps below its 2005 level and nearly
13pps below its 2009 peak (Figure 21). This does not
mean that households stopped borrowing: liabilities
continued to increase, but more slowly than nominal
economic output. Nevertheless, the contrast with the
public sector is striking. While government debt has
continued to rise, households have spent much of the

past decade strengthening their balance sheets.
But deleveraging was far from universal. Since 2005, the
debt ratio has fallen by 20.4pps in North America and
by 6.3pps in Western Europe, while Japan’s ratio has
remained broadly stable. Australia and New Zealand
stand apart from this trend: their combined ratio rose by
12.2pps to 113.8%, although it remains below its 2020
peak. The decline in the global ratio therefore conceals
a significant shift in the geography of household debt,

from the traditional advanced economies towards
emerging markets, albeit with important exceptions.
China recorded by far the largest increase. Its
household-debt ratio rose by 42.1pps to 59.4% of GDP
between 2005 and 2025, reflecting the extraordinary
credit and property boom of the intervening years.
However, the ratio has barely changed since 2020,
underlining how abruptly that boom has ended. China’s
household-debt ratio is now only around 10pps below

that of the US, but the two countries have followed
opposite paths: American households have deleveraged
substantially, while China has moved from rapid debt
accumulation to balance-sheet caution.
Elsewhere in the emerging world, the increase was less
dramatic. Since 2005, the household-debt ratio has
risen by 10.7pps in Latin America to 27.8% and by 6.1pps
in Eastern Europe to 21.8%. Both remain moderate by
international standards. Asia excluding Japan and China

also appears relatively stable, with its aggregate ratio
rising by only 2.2pps to 55.1%. But this regional average
is deceptive. It partly reflects the growing weight
of poorer, less-indebted economies and substantial
deleveraging in Singapore, where the ratio fell by
26.4pps to 51.6%.
At the country level, the fault lines cut across the
advanced and emerging-market divide. Switzerland and
Australia recorded debt ratios of 125.1% and 119.4% of
GDP , respectively, while Canada stood at 103.3% and

the Netherlands at 97.2%. Several Asian economies
also carry substantial household-debt burdens: Taiwan
and South Korea recorded ratios of around 92%, while
Thailand and Malaysia stood at approximately 87%
and 85%, respectively. These levels are comparable
with those recorded in several Western economies
before the global financial crisis. In 2006, household
debt stood at 98.4% of GDP in the US, 84.2% in Spain
and 95.0% in Ireland. High leverage does not in itself

signal an imminent crisis, but it makes households and
consumption more vulnerable to income, interest-rate
and housing-market shocks. The decisive dividing line is
therefore not between advanced and emerging markets,
but between housing and financial systems that rely
heavily on household leverage and those that do not.

Strong asset growth and subdued borrowing pushed

global net financial assets (financial assets minus
liabilities) up by +10.1% in 2025, broadly matching
the previous year’s +10.2%. They reached a record
EUR211.5trn, almost twice their 2015 level and more
than tripling their value in 2005.
China recorded the strongest increase in net financial
wealth at +15.3%, reflecting the combination of strong
asset growth and the near-standstill in household debt.
Latin America followed at +11.3%, while Eastern Europe

and Asia excluding China and Japan both recorded
growth of +11.0%. North America and Australia and
New Zealand also achieved double-digit increases
of +10.4% each. Japan recorded a strong +7.4%, while
Western Europe again brought up the rear at +5.2%.
Growth exceeded its 20-year average in almost every
region (Figure 22). Latin America was the only exception,
although its +11.3% increase was broadly in line with its
long-term rate.
The figures reveal a less visible source of wealth

creation. Weak credit growth may constrain housing
activity and household investment, but it also
strengthens private balance sheets when financial
assets continue to expand. In 2025, this deleveraging
dividend lifted global net financial wealth faster than
gross financial wealth. It also reinforced North America’s
dominant position: the region held 51.3% of global net
financial assets, compared with 48.1% of gross financial
assets. Low leverage relative to its enormous asset base

gives North America an additional wealth advantage.
Adjusted for population growth and inflation, China
remains the undisputed long-term leader (Figure 23).
Real net financial assets per capita have increased
more than sevenfold over the past two decades. No
other region comes close. Even the other emerging-
market regions recorded little more than a doubling.
Among advanced markets, Western Europe continued
to lag behind Japan, highlighting how weak asset

growth, rather than debt accumulation, has constrained
European wealth creation.
Comparing gross and net financial assets adds another
dimension. In China, net financial assets grew around
0.7pps more slowly per year than gross financial
assets over the past two decades, reflecting the rapid
accumulation of household debt. A smaller gap is
also visible in Eastern Europe. However, this is not a
universal emerging-market pattern: in Latin America
and the rest of Asia, gross and net wealth developed at

broadly similar rates. In the advanced economies, the
relationship is reversed. As household debt generally
grew more slowly than assets, net financial wealth
outpaced gross wealth. The difference was particularly
pronounced in North America, where net financial assets
grew around 0.7pps faster per year.

Examining the 25 richest countries by net financial assets
per capita at the end of 2025 reveals how significantly
debt can alter the ranking (Figure 24). Most notably,
the US ranks ahead of Switzerland to claim first place.
Although Switzerland remains ahead in gross financial
assets, its household liabilities of around EUR130,080
per capita are more than twice the US figure of
EUR52,730.
Taiwan and Sweden exchange fifth and sixth places.

Australia falls from eighth place in the gross ranking to
eleventh in the net ranking, while Ireland slips from 14th
to 15th, the UK from 15th to 18th and Norway from 12th
to 22nd. By contrast, Germany rises from 13th to 12th,
Italy from 16th to 13th, Japan from 18th to 14th and
Spain from 22nd to 21st. The Netherlands remains ninth
in both rankings. The comparison makes clear that gross
wealth tells only half the story: what ultimately matters
is how much remains after debt.

After a temporary acceleration to +4.7% in 2024,
growth in global real estate assets12 slowed again to
+3.5% in 2025. Apart from 2023, growth was weaker
only in the aftermath of the global financial crisis in
2012. Persistently high construction costs and elevated
mortgage rates continued to weigh on affordability,
transactions and new development. Overall, the value of
real estate assets in the countries covered by the report

reached EUR155.0trn (Figure 25)
Regional developments varied widely (Figure 26).
Eastern Europe recorded the strongest increase at
+10.6%, followed by Australia and New Zealand at
+8.5%. Both comfortably exceeded their long-term
growth rates despite the pressure from higher financing
costs. Japan also stood out: real-estate assets increased
by +5.0%, compared with a long-term average of
just +0.7%. Together with the recent acceleration in
household borrowing, this provides further evidence that

Japan may finally be leaving its long deflationary period
behind.
Western Europe recorded growth of +4.1%, slightly
above its long-term average of +3.5%. North America
meanwhile was the clear exception: growth slowed
sharply from +4.9% in 2024 to just +1.8% in 2025,
less than half its long-term rate of +3.9%. The global
slowdown was therefore driven primarily by the largest
housing market.

As with financial assets, a clearer picture emerges
when real-estate wealth is measured per capita and
adjusted for inflation (Figure 27). The long shadow of
Japan’s property crash remains visible: over the past two
decades, real-estate wealth per capita was essentially
flat in real terms, despite the recent recovery.
Real gains were positive in Australia and New Zealand,
North America and Western Europe, but generally
modest. Compared with financial assets, the gap was

particularly pronounced in North America, where real
financial assets per capita grew around 2pps faster
per year, and in Japan, where the gap was around
1.3pps. In Australia and New Zealand and Western
Europe, by contrast, the difference was only around
0.2pps annually. Property may provide stability, housing
services and diversification, but capital appreciation
alone has generally been a weaker engine of wealth
creation than participation in financial markets.

The country ranking of real estate assets nevertheless
shows how strongly housing can reshape the overall
picture. Switzerland had the highest real-estate assets
per capita at EUR350,450, followed by Australia
at EUR279,310 with the US taking the 7th spot at
EUR174,030. But what does the ranking look like when
net financial assets and real estate assets are presented
together? Figure 28 provides the answer. Switzerland
overtakes the US and claims first place, with combined

wealth of around EUR626,430 per capita. Australia
meanwhile is the biggest beneficiary, climbing seven
places from tenth in the adjusted net-financial-assets
ranking to third once property is included. Sweden
and Japan each fall six places, while Italy drops four.
Germany moves up one position to tenth. These shifts
expose two very different models of household wealth:
some countries build wealth predominantly through
financial markets, while others hold a much larger share

in bricks and mortar.
Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical offices, OECD, Allianz Research

International: Catch-up loses momentum
For many years, global wealth development was
characterized by convergence, as emerging markets
gradually closed the gap with richer economies. That
process has not ended, but it has slowed dramatically.
The ratio of average net financial assets per capita in
advanced economies to those in emerging markets
fell from 62 in 2005 to 21 in 2015. This means that, on
average, the net financial assets of richer countries were

“only” 21 times higher than those of poorer countries in
2015. Over the following decade, however, it declined by
less than four points to 17 in 2025 (Figure 29).
Convergence reversed from 2017. After narrowing
rapidly for more than a decade, the wealth gap widened
again between 2017 and 2021. Convergence resumed
in 2022, but at a much slower pace: the ratio has fallen
from 18.5 to 17.2 in the past three years. The global
wealth gap is still closing, but has slowed to a crawl.

Declaring the end of globalization would be premature,
but its character has clearly changed. Geopolitical
tensions, weaker cross-border investment and the
reorganization of supply chains are eroding some of
the advantages that emerging markets derived from
ever-deeper global integration. At the same time, the
green transition and AI could create new opportunities
by increasing demand for critical resources, energy
infrastructure, data services and digitally delivered

work. Whether emerging economies can seize these
opportunities will depend on their access to technology,
capital and skills. For now, these transformations are
unfolding in a more fragmented world, creating a less
favorable environment for poorer countries to catch up.
Against this backdrop, the global wealth middle class
comprised around 866mn people in 2025.13 Although
its size changed little compared with the previous
year, its long-term expansion remains impressive:

membership has increased by 81% since 2005. More
importantly, its center of gravity has shifted. Emerging
markets accounted for 61.9% of its members in 2025,
up from 43.6% two decades earlier, with China alone
representing slightly more than half of the emerging-
market middle class.
Distribution:
New wealth, old divides

The transformation is even more striking among the
wealthy. The global high-wealth class grew by 37%

to around 584mn people between 2005 and 2025.
Emerging markets accounted for 32.1% of this group,
compared with less than 2% in 2005. This expansion
came entirely from emerging markets, while the number
of high-wealth individuals in advanced economies
declined slightly. China played the dominant role,
accounting for more than three-quarters of the
emerging-market total.
Emerging economies also generated most of the
growth in the global middle class. Taken together,

they accounted for more than 90% of the increase in
the middle and high-wealth classes over the past two
decades. The global wealth landscape has therefore
become far more diverse, even though the gap in
average wealth between advanced and emerging
markets remains wide.
The global low-wealth class grew by around 7% to
4.3bn people, while the population covered by the
analysis increased by almost 17%. Its share of the total
population consequently fell from 81.7% in 2005 to

74.8% in 2025. Emerging markets still accounted for
92.0% of this group, slightly below their 92.9% share two
decades earlier. However, the number of low-wealth
individuals in advanced economies increased by around
21% to 346mn. The global shift towards higher wealth
classes is therefore unmistakable, but it has by no means
benefited everyone.
The emergence of a larger and more geographically
diverse high-wealth class does not change the fact that

financial wealth remains extremely concentrated at the
global level. This becomes clear when the population of
the countries covered by the report is divided into deciles
according to net financial assets.
In 2025, the richest 10%, with average net financial
assets of approximately EUR312,590, owned 85.4% of
global net financial wealth. Their share has declined
from 91.5% in 2005, but remains far above the average
national top-decile share of 60.9% (Figure 30). At the

pace of improvement recorded over the past two
decades, it would take almost another eight decades
for global concentration to approach the level currently
observed within the countries.
13  The classification in wealth classes is based on worldwide average net financial assets per capita, which stood at EUR36,690 in 2025. The global
wealth middle class („middle wealth“, MW) includes all individuals with net financial assets of between 30% and 180% of the global average. This

means that for 2025, the rounded asset thresholds for the global wealth middle class are EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. The „low wealth“ (LW) catego -
ry, on the other hand, includes those individuals with net financial assets that are below a EUR11,000 threshold, while the term „high wealth“ (HW)
applies to those with net financial assets of more than EUR66,000 (for details on how the asset thresholds are set, see Appendix A).

### Page 38

Allianz Research
38
High global wealth concentration is also reflected in the
wide gap between median and average net financial
assets. In 2025, global median net financial wealth
stood at approximately EUR2,490 per capita, while the
average was almost 15 times as high at EUR36,690. The
gap, however, has narrowed substantially over time: in
2005, the average was almost 33 times the median. The
typical person has therefore gained ground relative to

the global average, but the average remains a poor
representation of ordinary wealth.
At first glance, the much faster growth of average net
wealth in most lower deciles appears encouraging
(Figure 31). However, these high growth rates largely
reflect extremely low starting points. Even large
percentage increases translate into modest absolute
gains, while the richest groups continue to accumulate
much larger sums. A more equal global distribution of

wealth therefore remains a distant prospect.

The gap between the richest decile and everyone else is

enormous. The second-richest decile owned just 8.7% of
global net financial assets, with an average of around
EUR34,060 per person. The richest tenth therefore held
nine times as much wealth per person as the group
immediately below it.
The bottom half of the population covered by the
report, comprising almost 3bn people, held virtually
nothing. Its combined share of global net financial
assets was approximately zero. The bottom decile had
negative net financial assets of around EUR2,680 per

person, as liabilities exceeded financial assets. Even
in the two deciles around the middle of the global
distribution, average net financial assets amounted to
only approximately EUR3,170 and EUR1,810 per person.
Global wealth has become more geographically diverse,
but it has hardly become broadly distributed.

39
National: More wealth, same divide
Net financial wealth is distributed less unevenly within
countries than across the world. In 2025, the richest 10%
owned an average of 60.9% of national net financial
assets across the 57 countries examined, using an
unweighted average, compared with 85.4% at the global
level. The gap between average and median wealth
was also considerably smaller: the national average-
to-median ratio was 3.12, again measured as an

unweighted average.
When it comes to inequality, however, the national
picture is particularly disappointing. Despite being a
major political issue for years, there has been virtually
no progress towards more even wealth distribution.
In 2005, the richest 10% held an average of 60.4% of
national wealth, while the average-to-median ratio
stood at 3.11. Two decades later, both measures are
almost unchanged. Wealth has grown substantially, but
its distribution within individual countries has remained

remarkably resistant to change.
This overall stability conceals sharper shifts at the
country level. In 32 of the 57 countries examined, the
share of the richest 10% changed by less than 2pps
between 2005 and 2025 (Figure 32). Where larger
shifts occurred, however, the balance was clearly tilted
towards greater inequality, with deterioration almost
twice as likely as improvement. Wealth concentration
increased in 16 countries and declined in only nine.

Among those recording an improvement, Cambodia
saw the largest decline in the top-decile share, followed
by Portugal and Slovakia. Latvia, Hungary, Belgium,
Ireland, Malaysia and the Netherlands also registered
meaningful decreases. Different starting points were at
work. Portugal, Latvia and Ireland moved down from
relatively high levels of concentration, while Slovakia,
Hungary and the Netherlands improved from already
more balanced positions. In the Netherlands, the richest

10% saw their share fall by 2.4pps to 56.1%.
At the other end of the spectrum, China recorded by far
the largest increase in concentration. The share of the
richest 10% rose by 15.7pps between 2005 and 2025 to
68.0%, well above the average national share of 60.9%.
This accompanied the enormous economic and social
transformation of the past two decades, which created
not only broad-based wealth but also a substantial
private upper class. China illustrates the difficulty of

combining rapid wealth creation with an equally rapid
diffusion of its benefits. After all, it was the unbridled
growth of the „wild“ 2000s, when the private sector
was subject to few restrictions, that primarily caused
inequality to rise. In sharp contrast, in the last five years,
the concentration of wealth has remained unchanged.

The ratio of average to median wealth provides an
important cross-check: it shows whether rising average
wealth is also reflected in the position of the typical

household. Across the 57 countries examined, the
average ratio remained virtually unchanged, rising from
3.11 in 2005 to 3.12 in 2025. This confirms the broader
picture of persistent national wealth inequality.
The strongest improvements occurred in the
Netherlands, where the ratio fell from 4.04 to 2.48, and
Ireland, where it declined from 4.87 to 3.42. In both
countries, the narrowing gap indicates that the middle
of the distribution gained ground relative to average

wealth.
The largest deteriorations were recorded in Russia,
Sweden and China. Russia’s ratio rose from 2.99 to 4.77,
while Sweden’s increased from 3.77 to 5.25. In China, it
climbed from 2.03 to 3.33. Median Chinese net financial
wealth increased substantially, but average wealth grew
faster. China thus illustrates a central tension of rapid
wealth creation: prosperity can spread while inequality
rises at the same time.
More broadly, the limited movement in both distribution

measures across most countries suggests that wealth
inequality is deeply entrenched. Two decades of
economic growth, crises and political attention have
barely altered the underlying distribution.

China was not alone. The top-decile share increased by

Concentration also rose in several advanced economies.

decile share of 54.8% remained below Germany’s 59.5%.

economies, nor does rapid development inevitably produce
it.

Appendix A: Methodological comments
General assumptions
The Allianz Global Wealth Report analyses gross financial assets held by households, i.e. cash and bank deposits,
receivables from insurance companies and pension institutions, securities (shares, bonds and investment funds) and other
receivables, as well as household liabilities. Net financial assets are defined as gross financial assets minus liabilities.
Real estate is excluded from this measure and analyzed separately where data are available. The report is based

on data from 57 countries. This group of countries covers 91% of global GDP and 72% of the global population. In 43
countries, we had access to statistics from the macroeconomic financial accounts. In the other countries, we were able to
estimate the volume of total financial assets based on information from household surveys, bank statistics, statistics on
assets held in equities and bonds and technical reserves.
To eliminate exchange-rate distortions over time, financial assets and liabilities denominated in national currencies are

converted into euros using exchange rates at the end of 2025. Unless explicitly described as real, these values are not
adjusted for inflation. The closing date for data to be included in the report is 31 July.
Statistical distinctions
The process associated with the introduction of the European System of Accounts 2010 (ESA 2010) in September 2014
involved updating and harmonizing the guidelines governing the preparation of many macroeconomic statistics. The

new requirements also apply to the macroeconomic financial accounts. One change relates to private households: under
the ESVG 2010 regulations, the two sectors „Private households“ and „Non-profit institutions serving households“ are
now reported separately. This also has implications for the Allianz Global Wealth Report, which takes data from the
macroeconomic financial accounts as a basis where available. For many countries, however – particularly those outside

of the EU – there is no separate data available for these sectors in general. So in order to ensure global comparability,
this publication analyzes both sectors together under the heading „private households“.
Determination of wealth bands for global wealth classes
Lower wealth threshold: There is a close link between financial assets and the incomes of private households. According
to Davies et al. (2009) , private individuals with below-average income tend to have no assets at all, or only very few. It is

only when individuals move into middle and higher income groups that they start to accumulate substantial assets.
We have applied this link to our analysis. Countries in the upper-middle income bracket (based on the World Bank‘s
country classification system) therefore form the group in which the average household assets first reach a meaningful
level. This value marks the lower threshold for the global middle wealth class.
In terms of income, households with incomes that correspond to between 75% and 150% of average net income are

generally considered to constitute the middle class. According to Davies et al., households with income corresponding
to 75% of the average income have assets that correspond to 30% of the average assets. As far as the upper threshold
is concerned, 150% of average income corresponds to 180% of average assets. Consequently, we have set the threshold
values for the wealth middle class at 30% and 180% of average net financial assets per capital. In 2025, average net

financial assets per capita in the countries covered stood at approximately EUR36,690. Applying these percentages gives
the rounded thresholds used in the workbook: EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. Individuals with net financial assets between
these thresholds belong to the global middle-wealth class. Those below the lower threshold belong to the low-wealth
class, while those above the upper threshold belong to the high-wealth class.

14  Davies, James B. et al. (2009), The level and distribution of global household wealth, NBER working paper 15508.

Document 242: Allianz_2026-10-09_Toasted, not burnt France s bond sell-off and the road to the 2027 election_99a3202d

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-09_Toasted, not burnt France s bond sell-off and the road to the 2027 election_99a3202d.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-09
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

In Summary 
• France is the hardest hit in the global bond sell-off, mostly due to its own fiscal and political 
weakness. Since the Hormuz crisis began, 10-year OAT yields have risen 150bps (US +115bps, 
Italy +132bps, Germany +83bps) as the prolonged energy crisis exacerbates fiscal concerns in 
a politically fragile environment. Franc e’ fiscal deficits have remained above 5% of GDP for 
four consecutive years. Debt has risen from 98% of GDP in 2019 to over 119% in Q2 2026, and 
the 2026 deficit is now expected at -5.4% against a -5% initial target. 
• A "short Europe" trade amplifies the move and spreads it to the rest of the Eurozone while 
the ECB looks the other way. Foreign banks and non-banks hold 36% of OATs and Japanese 
investors about 5% (EUR137bn), exposing France to the repatriation trade and price-sensitive 
investors. Speculative shorts, hedge -fund asset -swap unwinds and thin liquidity feed a 
negative loop, w ith market depth more than 50% below normal and the OAT spread over 
Bunds peaking at 150bp s. The speed reflects a positioning shock, not a pure repricing of 
fundamentals. Long -term investors are sitting out ahead of the elections, given budget 
uncertainty and the sensitivity of OATs to political fragility. Spanish and Italian spreads are 
about 20bps wider, bank CDS are widening and French and Italian three -month bills are 
converging toward the ECB's marginal lending rate. Fragmentation is far from 2012 or 2020 
levels, but the ingredients are in place. The ECB has not yet intervened and may limit  itself to 
verbal intervention, while continuing QT and pushing through two more hikes (+50bps).  
• Baseline: A watered -down budget, but the fiscal arithmetic stays heavy for France .  
We expect GDP growth of +0.5% in 2026 and +0.9% in 2027. The Assembly votes on the budget 
on 20 and 27 October, and we expect a budget by year -end through ordinances or Article 
49.3, keeping the 2027 deficit at -5.3% of GDP. This should contain fiscal slippage and stabilize 
financial conditions, with OAT spreads in the 120 -150bps range. Higher rates  (+100bps) will 
feed through over the next three to six months, adding EUR25bn (0.8% of GDP) in gross 
interest expenses for households, non -financial firms and the government combined. The 
debt-stabilizing deficit is -2.7% of GDP, which requires savings of more than  EUR80bn, plus 
EUR15-20bn a year to stop social and interest spending from widening the deficit. This takes 
credible fiscal measures (better tax mix, higher retirement age) and growth -enhancing 
reforms. 
• Adverse scenarios: France-centered crisis or Eurozone crisis? The Middle East situation is far 
from contained; oil prices could stay above USD100/bbl for another six months , leading to a 
further global bond sell -off hitting France the hardest, tightening financial conditions and 
jeopardizing deficit reduction. Domestically, opposition parties could take down the 
government or slippage surprises resurface despite a passed budget, tightening conditions in 
the same way. In this context, GDP would stall at +0.0% in 2027 and spread s reach 150 -
175bps. The ECB would talk up and potentially launch repo intervention. In our view, it would 
take a substantial Eurozone -wide contraction (France -0.5% in 2027 ; Eurozone -0.2%; OAT 
spreads at 175 -200bps) for the ECB to tweak or stop QT, launch open market and repo 
intervention and stabilize funding markets (as OATs and BTPs make up 50% of euro repo 
collateral). 
• Looking ahead, the 2027 elections are the next test, and TPI is a late and uncertain 
backstop. With the presidential election on 18 A pril and 2 May 2027 (legislative elections 
likely to follow), we see four scenarios: i) a President with an absolute majority and allies, who 
delivers fiscal consolidation; ii) a President short of a majority who seeks alliances  (our 
baseline, with the 2028 deficit at -4.9% and spreads above 100bps) ; iii) a President short of a 
majority without alliances  and iv) a political and fiscal crisis, for instance from unfunded tax 
cuts or spending hikes, with spreads above 200bps and widespread contagion. Only in t his 
last scenario would the ECB activate the Transmission Protection Instrument (TPI). The risk is 
a lack of political will rather than of instruments, and redenomination risk is the tail case.

Allianz Research | 9 October 2026 
Toasted, not burnt: France’s bond sell-off  
and the road to the 2027 election

### Page 2

France is the country most affected in the global bond sell-off

Since the start of the Hormuz crisis,  yields on 10 -year French government bonds (OAT) have risen by 150bps  
(vs 115bps for the US, 132bps for Italy and 83bps for Germany), the highest jump among developed economies. 
The feedback loop between rising oil prices and interest rate expectations  has met structurally vulnerable public 
finances whose credibility has been increasingly undermined by growing political fragility since the 2024 dissolution 
of the National Assembly. France’s debt-to-GDP ratio has surged from 98% in 2019 to over 119% in Q2 2026, with 
over 121% of GDP expected in 2027 (vs 83% to 87% in the Eurozone). Meanwhile, the  weakening economy (+0.5% 
GDP growth expected this year)  is leading to a fourth consecutive  year of fiscal slippage : The government now 
expects a deficit of -5.4% of GDP against the -5.1% targeted in the 2026 Budget. With Moody's upcoming review on 
23 October, France is now at risk of losing its AA rating (currently Aa3 by Moody’s vs A+ by S&P and Fitch Ratings, 
i.e. already one notch below); current pricing, if sustained, would even imply a BBB rating.

Table 1: Macroeconomic and fiscal forecasts for France in 2027

Government Allianz Research 
Real GDP growth (%) 1.0 0.9 
Inflation (CPI. %) 1.8 2.2 
Public balance (% GDP) -5.0 -5.3 
Primary balance (% GDP) -2.1 -2.5 
Debt service costs (% GDP) 2.9 2.8 
Public debt (% GDP) 121.7 121.8

Sources: French government, Allianz Research

France is the first victim of the Japanese repatriation trade.  More than other European sovereigns, France's 
investor base is exposed to price -sensitive players. The most volatile group, foreign banks and non -banks, holds 
36% of French government bonds. Domestic banks and non-banks, whose holdings fluctuate less, account for 32%, 
while non -price sensitive investors (domestic and foreign central banks) make up only 32%. Other European 
countries have either a stronger anchor in the domestic market (Italy  with 66% vs 47% for France ) or with foreign 
central banks ( Germany with 26% vs 16 % for France as Bunds as the primary FX reserve vehicle). Among foreign 
holders, Japanese investors play a significant role , holding around 5% of outstanding French government bonds 
(~EUR137bn). As interest rates rise in Japan, these positions become less attractive relative to  domestic holdings. 
At best they are no longer rolled over, at worst they are actively liquidated. In the recent bond sell -off France 
became the first victim of the J apanese repatriation trade, even though it plays only a minor role compared with 
the US as an overweight in Japanese investors' benchmark allocations .

Figure 1: France’s risk premium is very sensitive to changes in political fragility

### Page 3

Fiscal concerns, political fragility and investor base have created the vulnerability ; sentiment and positioning 
determine the market reaction. A "short Europe" trade has built up for some time in markets and France is identified 
as the most vulnerable link (Figure 2). A negative feedback loop is now at work. A bearish narrative around political 
fragility and fiscal risk is amplified by very large speculative short positions, sales from Japanese institutional 
investors, hedge fund unwinding asset swap positions and a l imited presence of buyers, with market depth more 
than 50% below normal. In such conditions , even small trades can move prices sharply. OAT yields have spiked 
rapidly, with the 10-year spread vs the Bund widening to a peak 150bps, rising twice as fast as during the Eurozone 
crisis. Realized OAT spread volatility has jumped to the 90th percentile. These market dynamics point to massive 
repositioning rather than a repricing of fundamentals . But as long as a stabilizing force of buyers is missing, this 
volatility can persist.

Figure 2: A Short Europe trade has built up with pressure on EGB spreads and EUR -USD

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

As c ontagion has switched side s, the European Government Bond (EGB) market could move from country -
specific fragility to general fragmentation. The OAT sell-off has already spilled over to other EGBs. Unlike 15 years 
ago, when France fell victim to contagion from the periphery, it is now the other way round. Spanish and Italian 
bond yields rose significantly, while German yields fell. Their 10-year spreads versus the Bund widened by around 
20bps. This decoupling within the EGB space is accompanied by widening bank CDS spreads, especially in France. 
Meanwhile, Euro funding market is under elevated stress. French and Italian three-month bills are converging 
toward the ECB's Marginal Lending Facility rate, the effective ceiling for Eurozone money-market rates. A break 
above the MLF would signal a disruption in funding  markets. The ingredients for a broader fragmentation of 
monetary conditions are thus in place, even if the current magnitude  remains far from those of  2012 or 2020. 
However, monetary transmission is also a function  of time: If these stretched conditions persist for an extended 
period, an intervention of the ECB will be more likely , e specially if we see a persistent decoupling of yield 
movements between core and periphery that can feed fears of redenomination risk.

What’s next?

The 2027 budget bill is facing stiff opposition at the National Assembly, putting the planned deficit reduction in 
jeopardy. Presented to the cabinet on 1 October, the draft budget (PLF) and the social security finance bill (PLFSS) 
for 2027 target a public deficit of -5.0% of GDP, down from -5.4% in 2026. The budget is essentially about preventing 
the deficit from getting worse rather than a fiscal adjustment. The government has announced  EUR43bn of new 
fiscal-measures (1.4% of GDP) , split between EUR25bn (58%) in spending restraint (including slowing spending 
compared to expected trends such as healthcare spending, central government cuts, social spending restraint, local 
authorities cuts) and EUR18bn (42%) in new revenues.

First, opposition parties are already pushing back against most of the big revenue raisers measures, including the

### Page 4

cut to the tax allowance on pensioners’ income (EUR1.4bn), the taxation of sick leave (0.5bn) and cutbacks on 
funding to local governments (EUR2.5bn). Second, the persistence of elevated energy prices and tighter financial 
conditions could pressure GDP growth next year, denting expected government receipts. The government expects 
GDP growth at +1% in 2027, while we e xpect +0.9% but with increasing downside risks. The next key milestones to 
watch will be 20th and 27 October when the National Assembly votes on the Draft budget part I (revenues) and 
the Social Security finance bill (Figure 3). The debt stabilizing fiscal deficit stands at -2.7% of GDP, half of today’s 
level, which entails an improvement of the public deficit of EUR83bn from 2027 on top of around EUR15-20bn of 
additional savings to find each year  just to prevent the deficit from rising  (to contain rising social security and 
interest spending).

Figure 3: France’s key political & budget milestones

Note: legislative elections are not yet scheduled – the National Assembly’s term runs to 2029 unless the next president dissolves 
it. Sources: LOLF art. 45, Constitution art. 47, Assemblée nationale budget calendar of 15 Jul 2026, rating agencies, Allianz  
Research

We lay out different scenarios of what could happen between now and the elections (Figure 4). Starting in the 
short-term, we envisage three broad possibilities: i) a watered-down budget passed before year-end which plans a 
stabilization or a slight improvement of the deficit, ii) a political crisis  with opposition parties taking down the 
government and iii) a re-intensification of the energy crisis.

Figure 4: Economic, political and market scenarios October 2026-April 2027

Source: Allianz Research 
In our baseline scenario, we  expect the French government will manage to pass a watered-down budget while 
the energy crisis eases,  allowing the deficit to remain in check in 2027 (-5.3% of GDP) Opposition parties do not

### Page 5

have an incentive  to soften their stance with the Presidential elections approaching on 18 April and 2 May 2027. 
But they also do not have an incentive to create a political crisis by taking down the government. Notably, the 
National Rally (Rassemblement National, RN) has expressed a preference for implementing a budget rather than 
relying on the ‘’Special Law’’ mechanism that was used in early 2025 and early 2026 In this environment, we expect 
the government to manage to pass a mini budget, by year -end, of limited fiscal effort just to keep the deficit in 
check (at -5.3% of GDP), either through executive ordinances – a budgetary mechanism never previously utilized 
under the Fifth Republic – or by triggering Article 49.3.  
In this baseline scenario, OAT spreads should enter a phase of stabilization but contagion risk remains. After the 
clean-up of derivative positions, we see 10 -year OAT spreads trading in a pre -election range of 120 -150bps. But 
the evolution of the French spread will not be isolated , with the potential to trigger a renewed contagion move.  
The French budget and election situation feeds the “short Europe trade”, and this negative sentiment dominates 
country-specific fundamentals. We also do not believe that stabilizing the French situation alone will be enough to 
bring the EGB market back into a convergence trade. Now that dislocations have emerged, EGB spreads are likely 
to become the new playground for speculative (leveraged) players looking to exploit them. With this comes 
elevated rates and FX  volatility and the risk of money market stress. We expect the ECB in this case to limit itself 
first to verbal intervention like a change in wording from "monitoring" to "ready to act" or "disorderly market 
dynamics". We see the tipping point at a 10-year OAT spread above 150bps. 
In a more adverse scenario where the energy crisis does not ease and/or France slips into another political crisis, 
a crisis emerges, either France-centered, or with spillovers and contagion to other Eurozone countries.  Under a 
scenario of renewed tensions in energy markets, the global markets selloff would intensify. Financial conditions 
would tighten more in France, weakening the economy and putting in jeopardy the deficit reduction. Alternatively, 
the government could be taken down in Parliament, triggering a grave political crisis and leading to tighter 
financial conditions. Even under our baseline scenario of a watered-down budget, fiscal slippages could re-emerge 
– for example if the economy is weaker than expected, or if the fiscal adjustment fall s short of what is planned  
(e.g. if revenue -raising measures disappoint). Additionally, under a Special Law, the risk of wider deficit could 
increase if continued through the presidential elections  in 2027, which could lead to France -centered crisis or a 
Eurozone crisis if tightening financial conditions in France spill over to other Eurozone countries . In a France -
centered crisis we would expect the French economy t o enter a mid-sized recession as tighter financial conditions 
would weigh on domestic demand (see next section) , with GDP at +0.0% in 2027. In a Eurozone crisis , we would 
expect French GDP to contract -0.5% and the whole Eurozone economy -0.2%.  
In this adverse scenario, we could see the 10-year OAT spread rising to 1 75-200bps. This would create 
widespread contagion in the EGB market and in the banking sector. In this case, it will clearly be up to the ECB not 
to let the situation escalate into a euro crisi s 2.0. Unlike in the euro crisis, when the escalation was fueled by a lack 
of instruments while the political will was there, this time the deployment of the instruments (TPI, OMT) could fail 
due to a lack of political will and institutional inertia. It wo uld be particularly precarious if markets began to price 
redenomination risk, i.e. the possibility of a country leaving the Eurozone. But we see this scenario as a tail risk. If 
the EGB and money markets move towards general fragmentation, we expect the ECB to set operational steps (QT 
tweak, temporary operations) at OAT spreads around 175bps, but only alongside broader contagion with BTP 
spreads around 150bp s and Spain and Portugal widening together. For France, a QT tweak would represent a 
relief of EUR4.8bn on average per month, equivalent to around 20% of monthly issuances since June. But the 
problem is that “Europe short traders” can also do this math and adjust accordingly. At this point the ECB would 
not only operate in the secondary bond market but a lso need to stabilize funding markets as in  French OAT and 
Italian BTP represent 50% of the EUR repo market collateral. The TPI activation would be late in the process and 
require OAT spread around 200bps with widespread and persistent contagion.

In 2027, the presidential (and likely legislative) elections  could re -escalate economic and market pressures.  
We envisage four scenarios, which we detail more in Figure 5. In a nutshell, under our baseline assumptions, the

### Page 6

newly elected President will not secure a n absolute majority when she or he provokes legislative elections.  
Nevertheless, the President will have the strongest group at the National Assembly and will benefit from strong 
popular support. In this environment, we would expect the President to negotiate with some opposition parties to 
pass a budget putting the deficit on a downward trajectory. Nevertheless, deficit reduction will be limited ( -4.9% 
GDP deficit expected in 2028) amid strong aversion for rapid fiscal tightening in Parliament and public opinion. The 
10-year spread will likely remain above 100bps. In an adverse scenario of political and fiscal crisis, for instance 
triggered by the enactment of tax cuts and/or spending hikes  not credibly funded, a France-centered or Eurozone 
crisis scenario would re-emerge. In the latter case, the ECB would likely intervene with the TPI with French spreads 
breaking 200bps and Italian spreads above 175bps.

Figure 5:  Economic, political and market scenarios after April 2027

Source: Allianz Research

In the short term, a significant easing of global yields and elevated French spreads hinges on three factors:   
1) a substantial easing of the energy crisis, which would reduce inflationary pressures, limiting or even ending 
further central bank hikes. 2) Strong and credible fiscal -consolidation measures passed by decree, though this 
remains unlikely before the presidential election. One option used previously is  reducing healthcare spending, but 
the amount of savings is limited to EUR5-10bn (Table 2). 3) A cooling US economy could moderate the global bond 
sell off, though this too seems less likely before 2027.

Table 2: Measures the French government can implement by decrees to bypass parliamentary vote

Measure Can government act without new parliamentary vote? 
Cancel already authorized State spending Yes 
Freeze/cancel some budgetary appropriations Yes 
Adjust medicine reimbursement within regulatory powers Yes 
Adjust healthcare tariffs/payment parameters Yes, within statutory framework 
Tighten implementation of existing social schemes Often yes 
Change parameters of social contribution rebates  Sometimes yes 
Abolish/reduce a statutory benefit Generally, no 
Permanently change tax rates No 
Fundamentally redesign social-contribution exemptions Generally, requires legislation 
Change the ONDAM target itself Generally, Parliament/LFSS

Sources: French government, various, Allianz Research 
In the longer-term, only a sizeable fiscal -consolidation program can ease French spreads sustainably.  
Ideally, a combination of the following would have the most benefits:

### Page 7

Credible fiscal adjustment (the least growth -hurting). On the revenue side, fiscal consolidation should rely on a 
more efficient tax mix to limit negative growth effects. An increase of  VAT rates would bring large receipts without 
hurting potential growth. Freez ing income-tax thresholds should be preferred to increase social contributions to 
limit the negative impact on labor demand and labor supply amid already high labor taxation in France. Some 
inefficient and costly tax rebates and loopholes could also be cut down. But most of the fiscal adjustment should 
be operated through expenditure cuts. An increase of the effective age of retirement – for instance through a rise 
in the number of years of contribution – would bring substantial savings and increase tax receipts. Substantial 
savings could also be enacted on public agencies and local governments’ administrative costs. In parallel, the 
government could launch a comprehensive spending review to improve efficiency  of public expenditure, 
benchmarking what has been done in the past in countries such as Canada and Sweden.  
Growth-enhancing reforms. The government could implement structural reforms which will likely support growth 
in the medium -term, reducing the need for further painful fiscal adjustment later on. A pension reform would not 
only bring higher receipts and increase savings; it would also stimulate labor supply and potential growth 1. Other 
growth-enhancing measures include a revamp of school education met hods, scaling back excessive regulation, 
incentivizing higher-skilled education and hiring through a reform of the taxation system.

The trickle-down effect of tighter financial conditions

The ongoing bond market problem could increasingly become an economic problem through tighter financial 
and credit conditions, but the economy has buffers to sustain a short -term financial shock. Higher sovereign 
interest rates have started to filter through on to consumer, mortgage and corporate funding costs, and the pa ss-
through will continue through 2027. This will tighten the credit and financial cycle, through higher borrowing costs 
on new lending, higher refinancing bills and tighter access to credit, and ultimately weaken consumer and 
investment spending. Our BIS-built Financial condition index (FCI) for France shows that overall financial conditions 
have tightened sharply recently, but from a relatively loose level (Figure 6, left). They are now close to their long -
term historical averages. Besides, the credit impu lse for households and corporations remain s supportive to date 
(Figure 6, right). During the Eurozone crisis of 2011 -13, the credit impulse turned sharply negative but GDP growth 
held up. While the latest ECB Bank Lending Survey (BLS) points to the credit impulses weakening in the next couple 
of quarters, this does not necessarily mean that GDP growth will weaken sharply. The French economy has buffers 
to withstand a negative finance shock, provided it does not last too long and does not get worse. In parti cular, 
households have high savings and corporates’ cash reserves have been rebuilt recently, though many firms struggle 
with elevated bankruptcies.

Figure 7: Impact of a +100bps shock on interest rates for French households

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

For French non-financial companies, around 50.6% of debt is due to reset or mature within the next 12 months, 
well below the level in Italy, Spain, the UK and the US, where 78 to 90% is due for refinancing. However, France 
starts from the largest corporate debt stock in Europe in this sample, at around EUR2.27trn. This means that even 
with a lower refinancing share, around EUR1.15trn of debt still needs to be repriced within a year, more than in 
Germany, It aly o r Spain. In addition, the pressure is amplified by the starting level of borrowing costs. French 
corporates already face an effective interest rate of 6.1%, the highest among the European peers shown here. Under 
a +100bp s interest-rate shock, the effective pass -through would amount to roughly +89bp s, adding around 
EUR10bn to annual interest expenses. Relative to gross operating surplus, this represents an additional burden of 
about 2.1% of GOS, the highest impact across similar peers, and could shave roughl y -0.7pp off gross margins (see 
Figure 6). For the French government, a +100bps increase in the weighted average interest rates would mean +0.3% 
of GDP in 2027 (EUR 11.7bn) and +0.7% of GDP by 2035. Indeed, France has a rather long average maturity of its 
debt (>8.5 years) which protects it from very high volatility on its interest expenses.

Topics  Loss‑making firms are on the rise in China's growth sectors

In China, the number of companies operating in the red is increasing. And the rise is particularly striking in growth 
sectors. One factor behind the increase is the intense competition among firms, which has led to a situation in which 
only the strongest thrive. Another is that many next‑generation growth companies, despite being viewed as having great 
potential, are losing money.

■ Loss‑making firms are on the rise in growth sectors 
In recent years, the number of companies in China's 
growth sectors that are operating in the red has 
increased markedly. The share of loss-making firms in 
all listed firms (below, "loss‑making‑firm ratio") rose 
sharply from 9.6% in 2019 to 26.9% in 2025. Looking 
at the industry distribution of loss‑making firms, from 
2019 to 2022, the ratio increased mainly in 
non‑manufacturing sectors such as real estate and 
accommodation and eating/drinking services, 
reflecting the property market downturn and strict 
stay‑at‑home measures implemented to curb the spread 
of COVID‑19. Later, from 2022 to 2025, the 
loss‑making-firm ratio surged in 
information/communications equipment and 
electronic parts/devices; general, production, and 
commercial machinery; electrical machine ry; and 
chemicals. These four sectors alone accounted for 
around 60% of the overall increase. 
They are the principal growth sectors in Chinese 
manufacturing. This is evidenced by the fact that the 
growth rates of value added in all four sectors exceed 
the average for Chinese manufacturing. Many of the 
high‑tech manufacturing industries that the Chi nese 
government has prioritized for development are 
included in these sectors. For example, 5G base 
stations and semiconductors fall under 
information/communications equipment and 
electronic parts/devices. And robots fall under general, 
production, and com mercial machinery. Meanwhile, 
solar cells and batteries fall under electrical machinery, 
and biopharmaceuticals and organic chemical 
materials fall under chemicals.  
Looking at recent developments in the 
manufacturing of information/communications 
equipment and electronic parts/devices, the global 
communications standard began shifting from 4G 
(LTE) to 5G services in around 2019, and ChatGPT was released in November 2022. These developments 
have provided tailwinds for the growth of the sector. Recently, as it has become clear that ordinary 
consumers and companies can make use of AI services on a daily basis, demand for AI‑related devices and 
semiconductors, as well as d ata centers, has soared. In this environment, many firms are focusing on 
strengthening development, manufacturing, and sales, and the sector's value added has continued to 
increase, rising from 780 billion yuan in 2022 to 910 billion yuan in 2025. Yet over  the same period, the 
number of loss‑making firms increased from 113 to 198, and the loss‑making‑firm ratio jumped from 17.2% 
to 30.1%. 
■ Competition among companies means only the strongest thrive 
One reason loss‑making firms are increasing despite operating in growth sectors is the intense 
competition among companies. The fight for market share has led to overheated investment competition

2 
and price competition. In information/communications 
equipment and electronic parts/devices manufacturing, 
the rate of growth of non -current assets from 2022 to 
2025 (+35.4%) far exceeded the growth rate of value 
added (+16.9%). This indicates that supply capacity is 
expanding faster than demand. In addition, the 
consumer price index (CPI) increased by just 0.4% 
over the same period. Service prices rose 2.2%, 
reflecting wage increases, while goods prices fell 0.7% 
due to excess supply in manufacturing. 
Information/communications equipment prices 
decreased by 3.5%, larger than the drop for goods 
overall. 
As the selection of the fittest through competition 
among companies has progressed, profits have become 
concentrated at a small number of highly competitive 
firms. The share of profits earned by the top 1% of 
firms in information/communications equipment and electronic parts/devices manufacturing rose sharply 
from 24.0% in 2022 to 43.3% in 2025. Leading companies are simultaneously establishing positions as 
both "domestic champions" and "global champions."  
■ Next‑generation growth companies are viewed as having great potential even though they 
are losing money 
However, loss‑making firms cannot simply be lumped together and labeled as losers. Globally, investors 
have shown increasing interest recently in companies with high growth potential even if they are operating 
in the red, and China has a considerable number of such firms. 
In information/communications equipment and 
electronic parts/devices manufacturing, one out of 
every two loss‑making firms is a company attracting 
strong investor interest. The Shanghai Composite 
Index rose 28.5% from 3,089 points at the beginning of 
2023 to 3,969 points at the end of 2025. 20 of the 198 
loss‑making firms in 2025 were not yet listed in 2023, 
and if these are excluded, there were 178 companies 
operating in the red as of 2025. Of these 178 firms, 96 
(53.9%) saw stock‑price increases over the period 
2023-2025 that exceeded the rise in the Shanghai 
Composite Index.  
One characteristic of these 96 companies with strong 
relative stock‑price performance is their active 
investment in R&D and intangible assets. Their 
average R&D intensity (R&D investment / sales) in 
2022 was 9.6%, and their ratio of intangible assets to 
value added stood at 60.5%. Intangible assets include 
computer software, patents, copyrights, and 
relationships with customers and suppliers. In contrast, 
the 82 loss‑making firms with weak relative 
stock‑price performance had an R&D intensity of only 
6.5% and an intangible‑asset‑to‑value‑added ratio of just 38.9%. 
As the above has shown, the increase in loss‑making firms in China's growth sectors is notable, and the 
factors behind it include not only the selection of the fittest as a result of competition among companies but 
also the emergence of next‑generation growth firms that are viewed as having great potential. 
  (Shinichi Seki)

Topics  Climate change heightens the risk of growth stagnation in Asia

Climate change could elevate the risk of economic growth flatlining in the ASEAN zone and India by causing labor 
productivity growth and capital accumulation to stall. To mitigate or avert such a scenario, establishing a business 
environment and institutions that promote "adaptation investment" by both the public and private sectors is an urgent 
task.

■ Heavy‑rain and flood damage is becoming increasingly serious in Asia 
Huge downpours and floods 
resulting in human casualties have 
been a frequent occurrence in Asia in 
the past several years. Still fresh in the 
memory is the debris flow that 
occurred in August this year near the 
border between Nepal and China, 
which as of t he time of writing has 
been recognized as a major disaster 
with thousands dead or missing. And 
in November of last year, Southeast 
Asia and South Asia saw 
record‑breaking torrential rains, 
resulting in numerous casualties, 
particularly in Indonesia and Sri  
Lanka. 
One factor that heightens the risks 
of such floods and heavy rains is 
climate change. As temperatures rise, 
the amount of water vapor in the 
atmosphere increases. Therefore, 
global warming could intensify 
torrential rainfall. Although such risk 
is shared a cross Asia, the way its 
effects are felt differs by region. 
Within Asia, the ASEAN zone and 
India are heavily affected by 
monsoons (seasonal winds), with 
many areas experiencing concentrated 
rainfall during the wet season. In such 
areas, warming can furthe r intensify 
monsoon‑driven heavy rains, 
increasing the risks of floods and 
landslides.  
■ Climate change will delay the escape from the "middle‑income trap" 
Climate change not only results in human casualties by way of natural disasters, but may also undermine 
the long‑term economic growth potential of ASEAN and India through various channels. The Asian 
Development Bank (ADB) [2024] conducted a quantitative an alysis of the extent to which climate change 
will weigh on Asian economies over the long term. Based on the IPCC's "very high GHG emissions" 
scenario (SSP5‑8.5), the analysis assumes a 4.7°C rise in the Earth's average temperature by 2100 relative 
to pre-industrial levels, and estimates the difference in real GDP between that and a hypothetical scenario 
without climate change. The results illustrate the long‑term impact on economic growth through declines 
in labor productivity, stagnation in capital accumulation, and downw ard pressure on consumption due to 
sea‑level rise, floods, heat waves, and other phenomena caused by climate change.  
An examination of the results reveals that in every country/territory, the pushdown effect on real GDP 
relative to the no‑climate‑change scenario expands over time, with 2035, 2050, and 2070 each seeing 
greater impact. The pushdown effect for ASEAN and Ind ia is −15.0% to −8.8% in 2050 and −30.2% to

−18.1% in 2070, larger than the effects for China (2050: −4.8%, 2070: −10.3%) and Central Asian countries 
(2050: −1.7%, 2070: −3.8%). Within Asia, the ASEAN zone and India can be considered regions where the 
risk of economic stagnation due to climate change is relatively large.  
This may make it even more difficult for ASEAN countries and India to escape the "middle‑income trap." 
Although their income levels have risen, they still remain far from high‑income status. Indonesia, Malaysia, 
the Philippines, and Thailand became middle‑income countries in the 1990s or earlier, but have still not 
been able to join the groups of high‑income countries. Furthermore, Vietnam and India, which became 
middle‑income countries in the 2000s or later, have yet to enter a high‑growth trajectory like China once 
did. Climate change, by causing labor productivity growth to stall amid rising temperatures and companies 
to curtail investment due to more frequent disasters, may lead to income growth in the ASEAN zone and 
India being delayed further.  
■ Improving the environment to accelerate "adaptation investment" will be important 
To alleviate the pushdown effect of climate change on growth, expansion of "adaptation investment" by 
the public and private sectors is essential. Specifically, this could include improving working environments 
and promoting measures to lighten workloads a nd reduce headcounts in order to offset declines in labor 
productivity caused by rising temperatures, as well as developing infrastructure and strengthening facilities 
to prevent damage to production equipment from sea‑level rise and floods. However, ASEAN  and India 
cannot be said to have sufficiently 
developed the foundations for public 
and private promotion of such 
adaptation investment.  
As part of its Global Adaptation 
Initiative (ND‑GAIN), the University of 
Notre Dame (U.S.) evaluates countries' 
ability to use investment to achieve 
climate‑change adaptation in the form of 
a "readiness" score with three 
components: economic readiness, 
governance readiness, and social 
readiness. Respectively, these 
components assess 1) whether a 
business environment conducive to 
adaptation investment by private -sector 
companies has been put in place, 2) 
whether the effectiveness of institutions

and policies to support private-sector adaptation investment has been secured, and 3) whether tangible and 
intangible social infrastructure that promotes investment has been established. 
Looking at readiness in 2024, ASEAN countries and India generally rank in the middle to lower tiers 
globally, with a large gap between them and developed countries. Notably, the Philippines, India, and 
Thailand fall outside the top 100, indicating weak momentum for adaptation investment. Breaking down 
the scores, problems are especially evident in the economic and governance categories. India and Thailand 
have particularly low eco nomic readiness, ranking 113th and 124th, respectively, and five countries 
(Malaysia not included) fall outside the top 100 for governance readiness. Given this situation, to accelerate 
private‑sector adaptation investment, India and Thailand in particular  need to move swiftly to improve the 
financial environment to make it easier to procure necessary funds domestically and internationally, relax 
regulations to encourage the launch of new businesses, and reform labor markets to enable the smooth 
securing of human resources. In addition, for Asia as a whole, it will also be important to boost confidence 
in the institutional factors that enhance application of investment for adaptation by increasing policy 
stability, ensuring the effectiveness of legal systems, and curbing corruption.

(Tomohiro Hosoi)

Portfolio Considerations
We view recent volatility as a rolling
correction rather than the end of
the cycle. We favor diversified Equity

exposure, with potential opportunities
extending beyond Growth to Value,
Small- and Mid-caps, Non-U.S. Equities
(especially Emerging Markets), dividend-
focused and thematic strategies.
We favor adjusting portfolios toward
strategic durations given attractive
elevated nominal and real yields, and see
value in high-quality bonds, particularly
longer-dated municipal bonds.
Consider broader portfolio
diversification with potential
opportunities across industrial metals,

energy, Real/Tangible Assets, Hedge
Funds, private markets and global
strategies to capture themes from AI,
reindustrialization, infrastructure growth
and market dispersion.
All data, projections and opinions are as of the date of this report and subject to change.
I
N THIS  ISSUE
Macro Strategy—The  Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran: Bessent’s Three-Front War and
the Stakes for
Investors: Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury secretary, but
these days he looks more like America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches

from T
okyo, where he is trying to engineer a stronger Japan yen, to the U.S. Treasury
market, where he is battling to keep the 10-year yield from breaking  decisively above
5%, and to T
ehran, where he has become the point man in the escalating U.S. campaign
to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Ho
w Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A decisive victory on
all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets is next to impossible,

while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best laid plans. That said, however,
the U.S. Secretary of the Treasury has signaled that he is ready to take a very  proactive,
pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less encouraging news stems
from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the Treasury secretary.
Market View—Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”: Behind
ever
y Artificial Intelligence (AI)-generated response is a vast physical network of data

centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems, semiconductor facilities
and critical-mineral supply chains. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power,
water, advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and
intensify pressure
on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend
beyon
d AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
Thought of the Week—The Beauty of September: Call it an eventful first 13 trading

days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per barrel, diesel prices surpassed
$6
per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield breached 5%, and
the Federal Reserve (Fed) joined the European Central Bank (ECB) and the Bank of Japan
(BoJ) in hiking rates for the
first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has
shed 1.4% since the start of the month. It’s not unexpected that September would be
choppy for U.S. Equities, especially in a midterm election year. The good news is that

this has historically been an attractive entry point. Since 1950, the S&P 500 has never
been
negative in the six or twelve months post-midterm election, boasting average
returns of 13.8% and 15.8%, respectively. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election. A correction amid a strong fundamental economic backdrop
would be a
potential buying opportunity in our view.
